#2950. Google Imagines a Personalized TV Channel Full of AI Slop, Starring You

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Google's rejected patent application was for a TV service that made videos based on your viewing habits.

If you’ve ever dreamed of starring in your own TV show, this might be for you. Google’s latest patent application is for an AI-powered version of your personalized TV channel, using data advertisers have gathered about you. The AI-created shows can also insert you as the main character.

According to the website Patentlyze, the application is for a system designed to compile your video or TV watch preferences using genres and titles you watch on services such as YouTube, plus context from your IP address and demographic data such as your age. Although Patentlyze says this particular Google patent application was rejected, the company can amend the application and resubmit.

A Google representative didn’t immediately respond to a request for comment.

The system would include an option to make you the hero by building an avatar from your photos and replicating your voice for the lead character’s lines.

The program could create a personalized feed like “John’s Channel,” stocked with AI-made crime-comedy capers, rom-coms and sci-fi adventures, if those are the shows John enjoys. The service could make new videos based on voice prompts or create new ones on its own, the report said.

Building a channel of AI slop isn’t a new idea. Roku recently unveiled FairgroundTV, the first free, all-AI streaming channel.

Generative AI continues to draw widespread skepticism from both local communities and everyday internet users. New data centers built to power AI models have met growing grassroots resistance over their heavy energy and water footprints, while synthetic content faces mounting cultural pushback, especially from younger audiences, who have largely rejected AI-generated media as inauthentic “slop.”

#2949. Is Bitcoin Heading for $50,000?

The cryptocurrency has been on a downward spiral since the midterm elections took place earlier this month.

By David Jagielski, CPA – Nov 23, 2026 at 4:29PM EST

Leading cryptocurrency Bitcoin (BTC +0.69%) is down 30% since the start of the year. Last week, it also briefly fell below $60,000 as market sentiment soured amid concerning developments at Nvidia, which dragged the overall stock market down.

Bitcoin, which has often followed the movements of the broader market, has shown that it's no safer an option than other high-risk investments. With there being plenty of uncertainty still ahead and a potential for more rate hikes, could Bitcoin fall to $50,000 or lower before the end of the year?

Government regulation can play a key role in the acceptance and growth of cryptocurrency. It's worth noting, then, that since the midterm elections took place earlier this month, Bitcoin's value has dropped immensely. From around $77,000 before the midterm elections took place to around $61,000 now, the cryptocurrency's value has already fallen by more than 20% since then.

Investors may be concerned that, even with a seemingly pro-crypto government, significant reform couldn't take place. The Clarity Act, which would establish clear rules for crypto trading, failed to pass and may now be even less likely under a Democratic-controlled Congress.

To make matters worse, there's the potential for more rate hikes ahead. Next month, it appears all but certain that there will be at least a 25-basis-point hike, and an outside chance that rates could go up by 50 basis points. Interest rate hikes are bad news for speculative investments such as crypto. A big rate increase could indeed give Bitcoin a further push down in value.

Bitcoin is a highly speculative investment, and a lot depends on investor sentiment. Unfortunately, the appetite for risk lessens as interest rates rise. And without a catalyst on the horizon to increase crypto adoption, I don't see why things might turn around anytime soon.

Instead, it appears more likely that the leading cryptocurrency will continue to fall in value as investors grow more concerned about the broader markets. Bitcoin could fall to $50,000 or lower this year, depending on the extent of interest rate increases. However, even if they don't increase significantly, with plenty of concerns about the economy these days, there may be more investors looking to cash out of crypto than those looking to start a position in it.

I expect Bitcoin to fall below $50,000; it's just a matter of how quickly it happens.

#2948. Nvidia vs. SpaceX: The AI Revolution Is Big Enough for Both. Here's the Better Buy.

Nvidia and Space Exploration Technologies are two companies at the heart of the artificial intelligence trade.

By Bram Berkowitz – Nov 23, 2026 at 4:32PM EST

Few, if any, forces will reshape the economy in the coming years like artificial intelligence. Companies are spending hundreds of billions of dollars on the chips, data centers, and the models that power it.

Two very different businesses sit near the center of that build-out. Nvidia (NVDA +31.48%) designs the semiconductors that power and train most AI systems. Meanwhile, Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +4.35%) is working to become one of the key hyperscalers providing AI compute capacity, not to mention its plan to be a key pioneer in the space economy.

Both stocks have slumped from their highs, and Wall Street remains divided on their respective trajectories. While the AI revolution is big enough for both of these stocks, let's take a look at which is the better buy.

SpaceX runs three businesses. Its space segment leverages fully reusable rockets for space exploration, but it will also serve as the backbone of many of SpaceX's future ambitions.

But SpaceX's fastest-growing business, and the one with the most financial potential, is its Super Intelligence (SI) division. SI houses the social platform X, Grok intelligence, the company's data centers, and a future terafab facility.

SpaceX appears poised to eventually join Amazon, Microsoft, and Alphabet as a hyperscaler, powering the AI revolution. SpaceX reported 1.7 gigawatts (GW) of computing capacity last quarter and expects to reach 2.5-3 GW by year-end.

SpaceX founder and CEO Elon Musk also said on the company's recent earnings call that he's highly confident the company will bring an additional 10 GW of compute online in 2027 and floated a much larger figure as a possibility. Adding 10 GW of compute in 2027 would be an extraordinary achievement.

However, one could imagine that this kind of build-out will be extremely capital-intensive. SpaceX expects to spend $35 billion to $40 billion on capital expenditures in the fourth quarter alone.

SpaceX stock is not cheap, trading around 50x Wall Street's projected adjusted earnings per share. Analysts also expect revenue to jump from about $49 billion this year to $181 billion in 2027, nearly quadrupling. According to S&P Global Market Intelligence, Wall Street consensus estimates also project adjusted negative free cash flow of roughly $162 billion in 2027.

Meanwhile, SpaceX's longer-term ambitions, such as large-scale orbital data centers, depend on its fully reusable super-heavy-lift rocket, Starship, becoming operational and making regular trips into space.

No company has made a more critical contribution to the AI trade than Nvidia. Its chips serve as the lifeblood of AI models. Not only are Nvidia chips viewed as the best for general-purpose computing, but Nvidia's CUDA programming platform has been used by the majority of developers for nearly two decades.

CUDA can only be used with Nvidia hardware. This makes switching or developing programs on other platforms more difficult and creates a strong moat for the company.

Nvidia also continues to put up stellar results. Last quarter's revenue more than doubled to $115.8 billion, while the company's gross margin held near 75%. Management also guided for 90% annual revenue growth to $130 billion in the current quarter, topping analyst estimates.

However, the stock has been incredibly volatile since the scandal surrounding Jensen Huang came to light. Huang resigned on Nov. 12 following reports of multiple alleged extramarital affairs, including one reportedly with a woman who may have been a Chinese spy and who allegedly set up a network that illegally diverted Nvidia chips to China.

On Nov. 23, Nvidia's board of directors surprisingly reinstated Huang as CEO. A big question that remains is how much of Nvidia's prior revenue, if any, may have been illegally diverted to China. What could this mean for future revenue projections if regulators crack down on such sales, as they already have begun to do?

Lawmakers have opened investigations, and Nvidia plans to finish its own independent investigation regarding whether Huang was the target of a foreign intelligence effort.

While Huang's reinstatement triggered a furious rebound in Nvidia stock, it's still down 12% this year. There are still risks, depending on what new information comes to light or what investigations find.

But Nvidia still makes an industry-leading product in high demand among customers in the hottest industry on the planet right now. Even after the recent rally, the stock still trades at slightly over 10 times its projected adjusted fiscal 2028 sales.

While both SpaceX and Nvidia carry risks, and I wouldn't necessarily get overly aggressive on either, Nvidia is the better buy right now due to a valuation that has likely priced in a significant portion of the risk. SpaceX has tremendous potential, but I suspect it will struggle to balance growth with the need to continue raising significant capital.

#2947. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Mehta ranks No. 536 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.3%. See Chevron Statistics on TipRanks.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Scotto ranks No. 199 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.9%. See Enterprise Products Partners Financials on TipRanks.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

With more organic growth projects in the pipeline and new partnership investment opportunities, Dodge believes that BIP has clearer visibility into where it will deploy capital. That should support continued double-digit growth in funds from operations (FFO) per unit.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

Dodge ranks No. 476 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 68% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.5%. See Brookfield Infrastructure Partners KPIs on TipRanks.

#2946. Mortgage Rates Today, Friday, November 20: Noticeably Lower

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates eased today, but borrowing costs are still expensive for many home buyers.

Mortgage rates dropped today, but anything above 7% still puts pressure on home buyers’ budgets.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.24% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points lower than yesterday and three basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

A significant drop is eye-catching, but you should consider mortgage interest rates' overall direction, not just what's going on today. While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Next week, the Nerds are waiting for the Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) price index, due out Wednesday. It's the Federal Reserve's preferred measure of inflation, so investors will be watching closely. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates, but lenders anticipate its decisions, so mortgage rates often move before the Fed acts. Stubbornly high inflation is a big reason why we’re seeing upward pressure on mortgage rates right now.

If PCE shows inflation comes in hotter than expected, mortgage rates could climb as markets anticipate further Fed rate hikes. (An increase to the federal funds rate is already widely expected at the Fed’s Dec. 8-9 meeting.) A cooler reading could ease some pressure on mortgage rates for now.

I’m not getting my hopes up too high for meaningfully lower inflation, though. Last week's Producer Price Index showed wholesale inflation climbed 6.5% over the past year — its fastest pace in four years. Wholesale inflation measures the price businesses pay for things. When businesses’ costs go up, they could pass those along to consumers in the form of higher prices — keeping the flames of inflation alive.

Whether Wednesday's PCE brings relief or another helping of bad inflation news, we'll know before the turkey hits the table on Thursday.

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.74% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

#2945. Stock Market Today, Nov. 23: Nvidia Surges 31%, Adding Nearly $1 Trillion in Market Value as Jensen Huang Reclaims CEO Role

Nvidia's historic rebound has helped reverse some of Wall Street's recent losses, but questions surrounding regulatory scrutiny, export restrictions, and the sustainability of AI spending remain unresolved.

By RJ Fulton – Nov 23, 2026 at 4:40PM EST

Nvidia (NVDA +31.48%) staged a historic comeback on Monday, November 23, surging 31.48% to close at $165.05 following the board's decision to reinstate Jensen Huang as CEO and president. The announcement helped restore confidence in the semiconductor giant's leadership, adding nearly $1 trillion to its market capitalization and fueling a broader recovery across Wall Street.

The rally marked Nvidia's largest single-day percentage gain in more than two decades, reversing a substantial portion of the losses suffered during last week's semiconductor sell-off. Trading volume surged to 772.5 million shares, approximately 330% above its three-month daily average of 179.8 million, reflecting extraordinary investor interest.

Wall Street staged a remarkable recovery as technology stocks led a broad market rebound following last week's steep sell-off. The S&P 500 (^GSPC +4.73%) surged 4.74% to close at 7,238, while the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +6.51%) jumped 6.51% to 24,090, reflecting a sharp reversal in investor sentiment.

Semiconductor stocks were among the biggest beneficiaries of the rally, with Nvidia's leadership announcement helping restore confidence across the industry. Advanced Micro Devices (AMD +6.85%) climbed 6.85% to $453.46, while Broadcom (AVGO +3.00%) gained 3.00% to $313.55.

Nvidia's historic rally marks a significant reversal in investor sentiment following last week's steep sell-off. Jensen Huang's reinstatement removes a major source of leadership uncertainty, potentially restoring confidence in the company's strategic direction and relationships with key customers. However, the magnitude of Monday's rebound also raises questions about whether investors are responding to an improvement in Nvidia's underlying outlook or simply reversing the excessive pessimism that drove its recent decline.

Fundamentally, Nvidia's business remains exceptionally strong. Third-quarter fiscal 2027 revenue surged 103% year over year to $115.8 billion, while management raised its fourth-quarter outlook above Wall Street's expectations. These results demonstrate that demand for AI infrastructure remains substantial, even as investors increasingly scrutinize the sustainability of technology spending.

Looking ahead, Nvidia's reported SEC accounting investigation and export controls on semiconductors to China remain important developments to monitor. Additional regulatory disclosures or changes to export policies could materially influence the company's growth prospects and valuation. While Monday's rebound is encouraging, more progress is needed before a sustained recovery can be confirmed.

10 stocks we like better than Marvell Technology ›

#2942. College Scholarship Breakdown: Types, Sponsors and Eligibility at a Glance

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Scholarships vary in what they pay for and who can apply. Understanding the differences can save you time during your application process.

Scholarships have become a standard part of applying for college. You can learn about scholarships in a variety of ways, but looking through an online database of available scholarships — the most common way — can be overwhelming.

Before you go searching for a scholarship, it's important to understand the types of scholarships available and how to identify the ones that give you the best chance of winning, which can save you time and potentially earn you more funding. Here’s what you need to know about scholarships to decide which ones are best for you.

Scholarships are often funded by three groups: private organizations, institutions like universities, and government agencies. None is better than the other when it comes to awarding funds.

Nonprofit organizations and clubs that are looking for students either majoring in or researching certain fields, or who have experience volunteering in a specific field.

Companies that want to support all students or only fund those majoring in a field that relates to the companies’ industries.

Financial institutions, such as banks or credit unions, that want to support their community or their members.

Individuals or families that are funding private scholarships, usually in memory of a loved one.

Universities and colleges that are offering funds to students who attend their campuses.

Research organizations that want to fund students who will research in their areas of interest and further their causes.

These scholarships generally are provided by local, state or federal government agencies for a variety of reasons. Despite being funded by a government agency, the scholarships do not require you to pay back the money — unlike federal student loans.

Some scholarships pay for anything related to college expenses, including tuition, room and board, books and supplies, and more. Other scholarships limit what you can pay for with the funds, such as only tuition or tuition along with books. It’s important to look at this restriction when applying for scholarships to ensure the funds will go toward what you need to cover.

Merit scholarships require you to show excellence or achievements in your schoolwork, through talents and abilities, and even leadership or service work. Because these types of awards can be highly competitive, merit scholarships might require more than just achievements, such as volunteer or leadership experience. This means merit-based scholarships can overlap with other types of scholarship categories. Here are some examples of merit-based scholarships:

Merit scholarships often require you to have earned a minimum grade point average (GPA). The minimum GPA depends on the scholarship but often ranges from 2.0 to 4.0, with more competitive scholarships usually requiring higher GPAs. Scholarships with lower GPA minimums are more likely to consider additional factors, such as financial need.

Some merit scholarships might require a minimum ACT or SAT score instead of a GPA — or in addition to a minimum GPA.

Merit scholarships might also require applicants to have participated in activities outside of their schoolwork. This is common for scholarships that require membership in a certain group or organization. For example, membership in agricultural clubs, such as 4-H or FFA, and scouting groups, such as the National Eagle Scout Association, appear in scholarship requirements.

Some scholarships emphasize volunteering or community service and only consider applicants who have that experience. Applications vary in how detailed their requirements can be. Some scholarships want only a general description of where and how you’ve volunteered, and others might require experience with a service project that shows more leadership experience or time volunteering with a certain type of organization.

Scholarships that are awarded to students who can’t afford college are called need-based scholarships.

Unmet financial need is the difference between what it will cost you to attend college and what you can afford to pay. Because calculating how much you can afford is a complicated process, many scholarships refer to your FAFSA to document your financial need.

Some scholarship requirements may be as straightforward as demonstrating that you have financial need, but some scholarships might have income limits. However, income restrictions are less common.

Athletic scholarships are for — you guessed it — athletes. They are usually offered by colleges and universities to recruit athletes onto their collegiate teams and can be competitive. Which sports have scholarships available and the scholarship amounts vary by college.

Even though the main criterion for an athletic scholarship is being good at your sport, you’ll likely still need to show that you can keep up with your schoolwork. Most athletic scholarships require students to earn a minimum GPA or test scores to remain eligible while they’re in college.

Some scholarships are designated for students majoring in a general field, such as science or education, but others can be for very specific areas of study. For example, a scholarship for students majoring in a natural resource field could include majors in biology, ecology, wildlife biology, conservation education, forestry, soils and hydrology. However, a stricter scholarship might state that students have to major in more specific topics, such as biochemistry or microbial ecology.

Scholarships are available for active and former members of the military and members of their families. In addition to federal funds available for veterans to attend college, private organizations and some universities have scholarships for veterans to offset the cost of a degree.

Which family members can apply for funds depends on the individual scholarship. For example, some scholarships extend to children or spouses of veterans, but some go as far in a family tree as great-grandchildren.

Veteran- and military-focused scholarships might require that applicants or their family members enlisted in a certain branch, served during a particular time or conflict, or meet a certain rank or status such as receiving disability. Scholarships might also require that the applicant be a wartime veteran or family member of a wartime veteran; not all veterans meet this status.

Scholarships are also awarded to people who identify with a certain group. Common examples of identity-based scholarships include those for people who have disabilities, belong to certain ethnic or religious groups, identify as LGBTQIA+ or are first-generation students. Some identity-based scholarships require applicants to meet several criteria, such as belonging to more than one identity group.

Outside of what would be considered standard DEI groups, there are scholarships for applicants who identify with less common groups, such as veganism, homelessness and model railroad builders.

The best scholarships for you depend on your situation. For example, if you are a skilled athlete with a good academic record, going for an athletic scholarship might give you the most funding. On the other hand, if you’re going to research a topic that isn’t common, looking for scholarships that support your research topic might be your best bet because competition could be lower.

Good tactics to figure out which scholarships are worth your time include:

Think of the skills, talents, experience and identities that are unique to you. For example, being a child of a veteran immediately qualifies you for scholarships with fewer applicants than scholarships open to everyone. Likewise, if you embrace a certain ethnicity, identify with a specific group or have experience with a type of work, you might qualify for scholarships that appreciate and recognize those attributes more than the average scholarship.

Apply if you think you're eligible. Don’t apply to just a few scholarships and hope you get them. Instead, apply for as many scholarships as you can and for which you’re eligible. This increases your chances of receiving funds. Even small scholarships can make a big impact when you add several of them together.

Understand how you can use the money. If you already have a scholarship that covers only books, applying for the same type of scholarship isn’t helpful if you don’t need the extra funds. Instead, look for scholarships that cover the costs you have remaining.

Not sure where to look? Our sister site Scholarships360.org provides students with vetted scholarship opportunities. That would be a good place to start.

#2941. Stock Market Today, Nov. 23: Advanced Micro Devices Climbs on AI-Chip Rebound After Nvidia Reinstates Jensen Huang as CEO

On Nov. 23, 2026, investors rotated back into AI-chip names, with sector sentiment lifting shares across the group.

By Will Healy – Nov 23, 2026 at 4:50PM EST

Advanced Micro Devices (AMD +6.85%), an AI and CPU chips provider, closed at $453.46, up 6.85%. Shares rose after Nvidia reinstated Jensen Huang as CEO, and investors are watching AI-chip sentiment and conference commentary.Trading volume reached 53.0M shares, coming in about 93% above its three-month average of 27.5M shares.

The report alleged that some AMD server firmware fell into the hands of a blacklisted Chinese company.

Admittedly, at a P/E ratio of 98, the tech stock remains far more expensive than Nvidia, which, even with its gains today, trades at just 18 times earnings. However, analysts forecast that AMD's revenue will grow at 51% annually this year, then rise to 84% in 2027.

Amid a reinvigorated AI trade, such growth gives investors all the more reason to take a closer look at AMD stock.

#2940. Crypto Market Today, Nov. 23: Bitcoin Sits Out Wall Street's Historic Rally as $1.8 Billion ETF Exodus Weighs on Prices

Wall Street's dramatic recovery failed to deliver a boost to cryptocurrencies, exposing persistent weakness in investor demand despite improving sentiment across traditional financial markets.

By RJ Fulton – Nov 23, 2026 at 4:55PM EST

Cryptocurrency markets struggled to gain momentum on Monday, November 23, even as Wall Street staged a historic recovery following last week's sharp sell-off. Despite the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +6.51%) surging 6.51% and the S&P 500 (^GSPC +4.73%) gaining 4.74%, major digital assets remained largely unchanged, highlighting persistent weakness in investor demand.

As of early evening, Bitcoin (BTC +0.69%) was up just 0.08% to $61,213.38, while Ethereum (ETH -0.39%) slipped 0.09% to $1,719.49. Solana (SOL +2.27%) bucked the broader trend, gaining 2.84% to $72.57.

Cryptocurrency markets continue to face substantial selling pressure following last week's sharp decline, which pushed Bitcoin to a two-year low near $55,000 and sent other major digital assets to fresh bear market lows. Although prices have recovered modestly, the magnitude of the recent downturn has undermined investor confidence, with buying demand remaining insufficient to support a broader recovery.

Institutional capital flows have added to these challenges. U.S. spot Bitcoin ETFs recorded approximately $1.8 billion in net outflows last week, reflecting continued withdrawals from cryptocurrency investment products. These outflows have removed an important source of buying demand at a time when broader market sentiment remains fragile.

U.S.-listed cryptocurrency ETFs rebounded on Monday following last week's steep losses. The iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF (IBIT +4.85%) gained 4.85%, while the Fidelity Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC +5.08%) climbed 5.11%. Meanwhile, the iShares Ethereum Trust ETF (ETHA +8.01%) surged 8.01%, outperforming its Bitcoin counterparts.

The cryptocurrency market's inability to participate in Monday's historic equity rally raises concerns about the strength of underlying investor demand. Despite recovering modestly from recent lows, major cryptocurrencies continue to struggle with persistent selling pressure and the absence of sustained ETF inflows. Without a meaningful return of capital, any recovery will likely face considerable resistance from investors looking to exit positions established at higher prices.

For Bitcoin, the $70,000 to $75,000 range could prove particularly challenging. The sharp decline through these levels has left investors holding substantial unrealized losses, potentially creating additional selling pressure as prices recover. Ethereum and Solana face similar challenges following their recent declines, with both requiring stronger buying demand to reclaim lost ground.

With major stock indexes recovering sharply but financial conditions still restrictive, renewed weakness in equities could quickly spill over into digital assets. Until institutional flows stabilize and cryptocurrencies demonstrate sustained buying pressure, the risk of another leg lower remains elevated.

#2939. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2938. Dreading Holiday Money Talks With Family? Here's What to Say

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

How to prepare before you gather with loved ones, what to say, and what to do next.

Holiday gatherings have a way of stirring up difficult and downright uncomfortable conversations — and not just about politics or cousin Derek’s love life.

Relatives and friends may also bring up their money troubles and financial plans. That might involve asking you to lend money or your credit.

It’s easy to feel obligated or pressured to help out. But it’s important to establish boundaries so you don’t end up making a decision you’ll regret.

If you suspect a money request is coming your way, think carefully about what you’re willing — and able — to offer. You might choose a specific dollar limit or to give no financial help at all.

“Only lend what you're truly OK losing,” says Alvin Carlos, a certified financial planner at District Capital Management in Washington, D.C. “Look at your budget and decide what you could hand over and never see again.”

Choose the language that best fits your situation. Adapt your responses as needed to guide the conversation in a direction that works for you.

“I understand that you need help. I just need to review my own finances before I can give you an answer. I’ll get back to you by [day].”

If the request comes up at a holiday or family gathering:

“I’d rather discuss this privately. Let’s find another time to talk.”

“Here's what I’m comfortable with, and here's what I have to protect to be financially safe, mentally safe, and to continue to be there for you in the ways that you need me.”Or, if you want to be more direct:

Sometimes, there’s an unspoken expectation to give money because of how much loved ones think you earn, says Ashley Agnew, a certified financial therapist in Boston.

Getting ahead of it before there's a tipping point can help with tricky dynamics, she says.

A recent Edward Jones report found that only about one-third of financial caregiving arrangements were planned and discussed in advance. The report also found that nearly half of financial caregivers (49%) make financial sacrifices, such as reducing or stopping saving, or tapping into their retirement account.

Factor your financial health, your values and emotional well-being into your decision:

Review your income, expenses and savings. If you provide financial help and don’t get the money back, would you struggle to pay bills or reach financial goals? Prioritize building an emergency fund, saving for retirement and paying off high-interest debt first, Carlos says.

Consider your relationship. Would you feel resentful if you aren’t repaid? How do you think your friend or relative would react if you decline to help? Could it damage your relationship, and if so, is it worth it?

Understand the credit risks. Co-signing or allowing someone else to use your credit can leave you responsible for payments and expose you to potential credit damage.

Know the tax implications. If you’re considering a substantial gift or loan, check with a tax professional about any reporting requirements and tax consequences.

Consult your partner. If you share finances, make sure to include your partner in your decision-making process, Agnew says. If you have a parent asking for financial assistance, for example, but your cultural view is different from your spouse's, that’s a big opportunity for a rift.

Aim to be clear and direct. When in doubt, keep your answer concise, Agnew says.

“Keeping it short and sweet will communicate that you've made a decision, not that you've requested a negotiation.”

Depending on where you left the conversation, you might have more steps to take.

Stick to your boundaries and promises. Did you offer to contribute money? Pay as agreed. Did you say no? Don’t let your friend's or family member’s persistence, or the opinions of other friends and relatives, change your mind.

If you offered to provide nonfinancial support, Carlos recommends pointing your loved one to local government and nonprofit resources. That might include emergency rent assistance, cheap or free financial advice, food banks and career services.

Document the details and set the plan in motion. If you agreed to loan money, send your relative a quick text recapping the terms, Carlos says. He also suggests setting up a simple repayment system together, such as a recurring Zelle or Venmo transfer.

“Fewer reminders from you mean less tension,” he says.

Get outside help, if necessary. Despite your best efforts, it can be difficult to handle the situation on your own. If you’re feeling lingering stress or your family can’t reach a resolution, you might want to bring in some help, Agnew says.

She recommends working with a financial therapist or advisor, or using a financial wellness app.

#2937. Why Third-Party Risk Management Is Breaking In The Era Of AI

The security industry has never quite gotten the approach to third-party risk management (TPRM) right.

For as long as I can remember, TPRM has sat within the GRC function for most companies. For many teams, an annual questionnaire or a SOC 2 report was good enough to check the box, because vendors’ security postures would look about the same in October as they did in January.

That world is long gone. Vendors and Nth parties have emerged as one of the primary attack vectors for malicious actors. The common thread between the most notable headlines is third-party risk that nobody was monitoring in real time. The proliferation of AI-enabled attacks is only making the problem worse. ​

Mythos surfaced more than 2,000 previously unknown software flaws in seven weeks, with subsequent reporting placing the total above 10,000. Research also projects that the median time from vulnerability discovery to a weaponized exploit could fall below one hour by the end of 2026. The traditional human-led review cycle can’t defend this expanding attack surface that’s in constant flux.

The vendor ecosystems within enterprise environments are larger and more tangled than ever before. Traditional TPRM programs were built to track direct relationships. But the risk surface now extends far beyond the vendor named in the contract.

A company might approve a customer support platform, for example, only to find that the real exposure sits several layers deeper. The platform connects to a third-party analytics tool that relies on an AI model provider, which in turn uses a cloud infrastructure partner that becomes the source of a new vulnerability. None of those relationships would be visible in the original vendor review, yet any one of them can create exposure.

Security teams are tracking more tools, vendors and downstream dependencies than TPRM programs were designed to manage. The problem is only getting worse as the C-suite pushes teams to deploy AI tools in the pursuit of growth and efficiency. TPRM programs weren’t built to see that far down the chain, and it’s impossible for analysts to dig into the increasingly intricate web of third and Nth parties.

In other words, security teams are using human-paced processes to manage machine-paced environments.

The annual questionnaire produces a record of assessment. A SOC 2 report provides useful evidence about controls at a moment in time. A security rating can surface signals about external posture. Each input has value, but they have a dangerous commonality: They’re all looking back in time.

None of these data points provides a continuously current view of whether a vendor, product, AI system or downstream dependency is creating exposure today. Teams cannot make decisions based on stale, outdated data and expect to protect their business from current or emerging threats.

In response to this problem, some security teams might think the best idea is to speed up the existing process, but the underlying workflow is fundamentally broken. What actually needs to change is what the third-party risk program is built to produce and measure.​

​Instead of treating the initial assessment as the final word on a vendor, security teams need to keep asking whether the relationship has changed as that vendor’s role and access have changed. Maybe the vendor now handles more sensitive data, supports a more critical business process or has added integrations and dependencies that were not part of the original review. Any of those changes can alter the risk profile.​

Vendor concentration risk is another area that is easy to miss. Several important vendors may rely on the same software component, AI model or infrastructure service. Looking at those vendors one at a time can make each relationship appear manageable, while the shared dependency creates a much larger exposure across the business.​

Teams also need to be clear about what should trigger an escalation. A new dependency, a major vulnerability or a change in how the business uses a vendor may signal the need for more scrutiny. Deciding in advance who owns that reassessment and what information they need can make it much easier to respond when conditions change.​

It’s the context that surfaces from this new approach to TPRM that will help security teams make better decisions. In practice, that means understanding how a vendor touches the business. A payroll provider, identity platform, customer support tool and experimental AI app all create different kinds of exposure. The exact same vulnerability can introduce very different levels of risk depending on what data the third party impacted touches, what workflows they’re embedded in and your organization’s ability to keep operating if that provider is disrupted.​

The questions CISOs should be asking now are different than they were just a few years ago. How current is our view of our most critical vendors? What are our weakest links across our entire supply chain? What concentration risk do we have from fourth or fifth parties? Do we know where AI capabilities have entered the vendor ecosystem? Can we connect a new vulnerability to the exact business processes and data that may be exposed? Are GRC and security operations working from the same live picture, or are they still managing risk on separate clocks?​

This is where TPRM is heading. ​A function that has historically centered on point-in-time compliance reviews is transforming into a continuous security discipline. The Mythos era has accelerated that shift by introducing autonomous offensive capabilities that move faster than manual review cycles can keep pace with. Security teams need the same continuous visibility and increasingly autonomous defenses across the software supply chain.​​​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#2935. Prediction: This Will Be Sandisk’s Stock Price by the End of 2027

Massive revenue growth and strengthening headwinds in AI will likely define the memory giant's destiny over the next year.

By Will Healy – Nov 23, 2026 at 4:56PM EST

Sandisk (SNDK +10.69%) has been one of the more notable tech growth stories over the last two years. After Western Digital spun it back off in February of last year, the stock rose by as much as 6,400%.

Unfortunately, the stock began a dramatic downturn from that peak over the summer, and it’s now down by more than 70% from its high. That did not stop Sandisk management from initiating an 8-for-1 stock split that took place Friday. Nonetheless, that volatility calls the stock's state into question, particularly when you’re trying to predict where it will trade at the end of 2027.

When comparing the company's continued growth and the expectations surrounding its industry, I believe Sandisk stock could end 2027 in the $170-per-share range.

Sandisk operates in one of the most volatile parts of the tech industry: memory. It specializes in NAND, a non-volatile type of memory that is used for longer-term storage, as well as in solid-state drives.

Western Digital bought the company back in 2016, but since it spun it back off again as a separate company, Sandisk has prospered. The rapid surge in AI data center construction has led to a severe shortage of the types of memory it supplies, giving Sandisk unusually strong pricing power. The result has been soaring revenues, wide profit margins, and a much higher stock price.

Unfortunately for investors, memory-chip makers are among the most volatile players in the chip industry. Memory products are fairly commoditized — there’s not a lot of differentiation between the NAND made by Sandisk and that sold by its direct peers. For this reason, prices are tightly linked to the supply-and-demand dynamic. Eventually, supply will catch up to demand and Sandisk will lose much of its pricing power.

Additionally, despite rapid growth, headwinds in the AI industry are emerging as investors question whether it can maintain current levels of capital expenditures (capex) amid rising interest rates. If capital becomes more scarce, the pace of the AI build-out could slow.

All of those conditions make it difficult to tell whether Sandisk is a bargain or a value trap.

Right now, Sandisk trades at around 5 times earnings. However, memory stock investors know the both revenue and profits can reverse dramatically during an industry downturn. History suggests that investors should not perceive that valuation as a "bargain."

Despite those risks, Sandisk's immediate future appears too compelling to dismiss.

In its fiscal 2027, which will end in July 2027, analysts forecast it will bring in just under $50 billion in revenue. If that prediction proves accurate, it will amount to a 145% year-over-year increase. Analysts also expect net earnings of $28.44 per share. On a split-adjusted basis, it earned $8.86 per share in its fiscal 2026.

Looking forward into fiscal 2028, analysts forecast revenue growth will slow to 24%. That could generate negative sentiment, but the $36.13 in consensus earnings expected that year would leave the P/E ratio at 2.4 at the current share price of $79 per share. That appears unreasonably low.

Nonetheless, whatever the sentiment, $28.44 per share multiplied by 6 — a common earnings multiple for memory stocks — would leave Sandisk with an approximate price of $170 per share. That factor alone makes it difficult to believe that Sandisk will not rise again.

Considering the forces that will likely pull Sandisk in opposing directions, I believe the stock will end 2027 in the neighborhood of $170, and trading at a P/E of about 6.

Admittedly, with the stock seemingly in free fall now, this might be a controversial call. The pricing power that Sandisk commands for now could disappear if the AI bubble bursts.

However, for now, an AI bust is more fear than reality. Additionally, we know that Sandisk’s bottom line will be far higher by the end of 2027. Unless the industry completely falls apart, it is hard to imagine that Sandisk stock will not trade at a much higher price by the end of next year.

#2930. Mortgage Rates Today, Monday, November 23: Decidedly Higher

TL;DR: Surging tech stocks aren't great news for bonds, or for mortgage rates.

Mortgage rates have had some substantial day-over-day swings lately, and today, unfortunately it's an upward swing.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage jumped to 7.38% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points higher than yesterday and four basis points higher than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Recently news about big tech has often pulled rates down. Investor concerns about AI profitability keep cropping up, and when they do, tech stocks and bonds lose their appeal and Treasuries have been picking up the slack. Mortgage rates are generally benchmarked to the yield on the 10-year Treasury, and Treasury prices and yields move inversely. More demand for the 10YT drives prices up, and sends yields (and thus, mortgage rates) down.

Today's the opposite, though. Investors are having a bout of tech exuberance as Jensen Huang got reinstated as Nvidia CEO, and lots of excitement about tech stocks and bonds means less enthusiasm for Treasuries.

Here's the thing, though: Markets are fickle. They also move fast. And they aren't the only influence on mortgage rates.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context. For more, keep reading below the charts.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Lately, we've seen two narratives influencing mortgage rates battling it out; depending on which one's stronger, daily rates move higher or lower. Up top I talked about how shifting perceptions of big tech have been driving mortgage rates (and yes, today, pushing them higher, though usually it's pulled rates down.) When rates are moving higher, more often it's been thanks to inflation — and while it's a lightweight work week, we're about to get heavyweight inflation news.

The latest Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) price index is due out Wednesday. It's the Federal Reserve's preferred measure of inflation, so investors will be watching closely.

The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates, but its decisions influence the entire economy. That includes mortgage lenders, who tend to adjust rates ahead of its decisions. Raising the federal funds rate (the short-term borrowing rate the central bankers actually set) is the Fed's main tactic for managing inflation, so you can guess how things are likely to go at the Fed's December meeting. Markets already feel fairly certain about a quarter-percent rate hike.

We already know how PCE's likely to go, since it's the last of the three big inflation measures to be released. We've already gotten last month's Consumer Price Index and Producer Price Index. October's CPI came in worse than predicted, showing widespread price increases. (When most of the increase is driven by one area, it's easier to be optimistic that inflation can be contained.) And October’s PPI showed wholesale inflation climbed at its fastest pace in almost four years. PPI measures the price businesses pay for things, so it's often a preview of what consumers could face once those costs get passed along.

Predictions for October PCE aren't great, so at this point the hope is that the numbers aren't even worse than expected. And again, PCE's out Wednesday morning. We'll know whether we're getting seconds (actually, thirds) of bad October inflation news well before the turkey hits the table on Thursday.

And speaking of: Markets will be closed on Thanksgiving, and Black Friday's a half day, so we'll all have a bit of a break this week. Mortgage rates don't move much when the markets aren't moving. I won't be here to talk to you about mortgage rates, either, 'cause NerdWallet gives us Thursday and Friday off. Happy Thanksgiving!

Jason and Lucia would have to change a lot about their lifestyle to qualify (we know they're working on building credit, but having all your income be cash you can't really document is not going to work for a lender). But hey, I'm rooting for them.

With rates where they are right now, you could start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.88% or higher — but there just aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

#2928. As Tech Stocks Crash, Is Berkshire Hathaway a Safe-Haven Investment?

Berkshire's stock has been rising while tech stocks have been falling.

By David Jagielski, CPA – Nov 23, 2026 at 4:59PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Berkshire Hathaway ›

Although Warren Buffett no longer runs Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA +0.49%)(BRKB +0.41%), the company still operates with the same investing principles, focusing its efforts on high-quality investments.

At a time when the markets have been shaky of late, particularly pertaining to the tech sector, Berkshire has been a stock that's been rising in value lately. Could it be a safe place for investors to park their money, amid the uncertainty in not only tech but also the broader markets?

Tech stocks have been doing well for the most part this year, as the State Street Technology Select Sector SPDR ETF has risen by around 13%, which is in line with Berkshire's performance -- it's up around 14%.

But what's notable is the shift over the past three months. Berkshire's stock has risen by 16% during that stretch, while the Tech-focused ETF has gone in the opposite direction, falling by 11%. It could be a sign of investors pivoting to safer stocks, such as Berkshire, in the wake of all the turmoil involving tech of late.

Berkshire is, after all, a prototypical value stock that doesn't take on much risk and which can provide investors with excellent value. However, under new CEO Greg Abel, I've noticed the company investing more in tech, including Bending Spoons, a serial acquirer I couldn't imagine Buffett investing in. But it is a minor position in the overall portfolio.

By and large, Berkshire's focus remains unchanged, with close to half of its portfolio centered around three large, iconic companies: Apple, American Express, and Coca-Cola.

With stalwarts such as those centering its portfolio, the stock can indeed be a safe option for investors. But whether it's the best option is certainly debatable.

There are a couple of reasons I wouldn't buy Berkshire's stock for safety. The first is that it doesn't pay a dividend, which can be crucial when investors want to maximize returns amid uncertainty. The second is that at 16 times earnings, the stock is a bit more expensive than normal.

Ultimately, with markets in a bit of a downturn of late, I think investors can find better options, such as discounted dividend stocks that at least offer decent yields. Berkshire can be a good safe-haven investment, but before buying the stock, investors may want to consider other options.

#2926. Ground Truth: The Unsung Foundation Of Industrial Autonomy

Ronald Bisio is Group President, Field Systems, at Trimble.

​While the public’s attention is captivated by self-driving vehicles, city robotaxis and visions of flying cars, there’s a more consequential autonomous evolution taking place across heavy industry.

In building and infrastructure construction, autonomy is a navigation and execution challenge. A 50-ton excavator doesn’t just travel across a construction site from Point A to Point B. It digs trenches, grades slopes and moves thousands of tons of earth to match a complex 3D digital model down to the centimeter. A commercial freight truck hauling 80,000 pounds along a highway corridor isn’t just following traffic. With advanced digital mapping and precise positioning technologies, the truck holds a precise spatial line through torrential rain, dense fog or featureless stretches of road where cameras lose contrast.

​In the physical world, knowing what’s immediately around a machine is critical, but a real breakthrough happens when we connect local perception through cameras, LiDAR and radar to spatial ground truth, an invisible infrastructure that complements onboard sensors. This is the high-precision global positioning that uses an absolute coordinate frame of latitude, longitude, elevation and time down to the centimeter. When we connect local perception with precise positioning, we close the gap between machinery observing what’s around and knowing exactly where the machinery sits on the physical earth.

With these technological advances in heavy industry, there’s an understandable anxiety about automation replacing human workers. But as I visit customers and jobsites, I see the opposite. Across construction and transportation, the shortage of skilled workers is evident. Contractors can’t find enough experienced workers to meet infrastructure construction demands, and fleet owners are facing historically high driver turnover.

​Fundamentally, industrial autonomy is about elevating human activity and decision making. By putting high-precision digital guidance into a cab, an inexperienced operator can perform complex grading work. Instead of performing repetitive manual layout, workers can oversee connected equipment, manage exceptions and focus on site safety. Autonomous equipment and robotics help automate repetitive and hazardous operations, freeing human talent to focus on higher-order tasks.

​Over the past few years, perception sensors have improved dramatically. Cameras, LiDAR and radar give machines exceptional eye-level vision so they can recognize nearby obstacles, workers and vehicles.

​But vision sensors have two inherent vulnerabilities. First, these optical systems may degrade in harsh environments and be rendered momentarily blind by factors like heavy dust, glare or dark tunnels. Second, vision sensors lack global context. A camera can spot an obstacle five feet away, but it can’t tell a bulldozer where it sits on the earth’s surface.​

To address this, machines may rely on satellite positioning, but they also must be on guard for the illusion of precision. Driven by the rise of low-cost radios, increasing radio frequency interference leaves satellite signals vulnerable to both accidental jamming and deliberate spoofing. Spoofing is particularly dangerous because a legacy GPS receiver may lock onto a falsified signal and output a position with high mathematical confidence, even though the machine is actually several meters off target.

​If a mapping app gets bad location data, a passenger vehicle driver might make a wrong turn or miss an exit. But when heavy equipment is shaping a foundation or grading a road, inaccurate location data may cause costly rework, wasted materials and safety risks.

Rather than jumping straight to driverless machines on jobsites, we’re seeing a steady advancement of smart equipment guidance and operator assistance. Recent milestones in the field demonstrate the impact:​

In the recent building of a new EV manufacturing facility, Landmark Construction, a civil contractor utilizing our positioning solutions, orchestrated more than 150 machine-guided excavators, dozers and graders connected to a single 3D digital site blueprint. Guided by continuous satellite positioning, the machines automatically adjusted blades and buckets to match design lines. By eliminating six or seven manual data transfer steps between the field and the office, earthmoving tasks that previously took a month were completed in several days—and often with more accuracy.

High-precision positioning is steadily moving out of specialized niches and into mainstream production of electric vehicles and commercial long-haul trucks. Fusing satellite data with vehicle movement sensors allows passenger cars and big trucks to maintain lane-level accuracy in urban canyons and bad weather, giving automated driver-assist systems the required reliability.

In the air, autonomous drones are opening up new options for delivering urgent medical supplies. Guided by resilient positioning technology, these delivery drones can maintain their flight accuracy and operate safely despite localized signal degradation, bad weather and magnetic interference.

Technology leaders considering autonomous solutions for their operations should keep these three principles in mind:

1. Precision is the bridge to physical reality. A few inches of spatial error may not matter in the virtual world, but on active jobsites, centimeter-level precision is what’s needed for accurate physical execution on the first pass.

2. Spatial trust is necessary for safe autonomy. Heavy machinery can’t alter the physical world without verified, uncompromised positioning. Precision is the core safety architecture for real-world automation.

3. Accuracy amplifies human potential. High-precision guidance elevates rather than replaces human judgment. Precision technology takes guesswork out of complex physical tasks so less experienced workers can operate like skilled veterans.

​Industrial autonomy aims to seamlessly connect digital design and physical reality. A backbone of high-precision positioning opens up new possibilities for pairing intelligent machinery with human expertise. We only need to dream them into reality.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#2922. Stock Market Today, Nov. 23: Micron Surges 19% as Nvidia CEO Change Calms AI Spending Fears

Today, Nov. 23, 2026, memory chipmaker Micron's rally came on volume well above its three-month average.

By RJ Fulton – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:00PM EST

Micron Technology (MU +19.04%) surged 19.04% on Monday, November 23, closing at $707.70 as semiconductor stocks staged a remarkable recovery following last week's steep sell-off. Nvidia's decision to reinstate Jensen Huang as CEO helped restore confidence across the artificial intelligence (AI) industry, while commentary from Citi suggesting concerns over AI infrastructure spending may be overblown provided additional support for memory-chip stocks. Trading volume reached 49.1 million shares, approximately 72% above its three-month daily average of 28.6 million, highlighting strong investor participation in the rebound.

Led by semiconductor stocks, Wall Street staged a broad rebound following last week's steep sell-off. The S&P 500 (^GSPC +4.73%) surged 4.74% to close at 7,238, while the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC +6.51%) jumped 6.51% to 24,090, reflecting a sharp improvement in investor sentiment.

The rally extended across the semiconductor industry, particularly among companies exposed to growing demand for AI infrastructure. Western Digital (WDC +13.84%) climbed 13.84% to $297.25, while NXP Semiconductors (NXPI +8.88%) gained 8.93% to $256.14. The widespread gains suggest investors are reassessing the outlook for chipmakers following a period of intense selling pressure and concerns surrounding AI-related capital spending.

Micron's sharp rebound highlights the market's continued sensitivity to developments surrounding artificial intelligence infrastructure spending. Although uncertainty around Nvidia's leadership has eased, the underlying demand for advanced memory remains the more important factor for Micron's long-term outlook. As AI models become increasingly complex and data centers require greater computing capacity, demand for high-performance memory could provide a substantial growth opportunity.

However, the memory semiconductor industry remains highly cyclical, with profitability heavily influenced by supply, demand, and pricing conditions. Following Monday's 19% rally, investors should be particularly mindful of whether Micron's earnings growth can justify its valuation, especially if major technology companies begin moderating their AI capital expenditures.

Looking ahead, Micron's appearance at the UBS Global Technology and AI Conference and its December 17 earnings report will provide important opportunities to assess these risks. Investors should focus on management's commentary regarding memory pricing, production capacity, and expected demand from AI customers. Sustained pricing strength and improving profitability would provide stronger evidence that Micron's recovery is supported by fundamentals rather than simply a rebound in semiconductor stocks.
