#2918. Fed Chair Kevin Warsh Just Laid Out 3 Scenarios for "Super Intelligence." 2 of Them Are "Less Positive," Including 1 Scenario That Outright Disappoints

The Federal Reserve has begun modeling the projected impacts of what the Trump administration calls "super intelligence" on the economy.

By Bram Berkowitz – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:00PM EST

As chair of the Federal Reserve's Board of Governors, Kevin Warsh has a powerful job. His words can move markets, and his view of the economy can play a big role in shaping the policy decisions that follow.

That's why his thinking on artificial intelligence, or what Warsh refers to as “super intelligence,” matters. Super intelligence (SI) is the term adopted by President Donald Trump and his administration to describe AI, and has become a driving force of both corporate spending and stock market gains. Whether it delivers on its promise could impact growth, jobs, and inflation for years. These are the very concerns the Fed is charged with managing, so it has to try to figure out what's coming before it arrives.

On Nov. 16, at a conference for the Fed's Productivity and Jobs Task Force hosted by the Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco, Warsh said the Fed is modeling three possible paths for the economy as AI continues to develop.

Two of these scenarios are "less positive," including one where artificial intelligence outright disappoints.

Image source: Official Federal Reserve Photo.

During his opening remarks, Warsh said Fed staff had built three illustrative scenarios over the next several years, stressing that they are not forecasts and that he was not there to offer predictions.

The first is the one that every investor hopes for. Productivity rises, and the gains are shared evenly between workers and those who manage and own capital. In this scenario, growth remains above its long-run average, and the economy remains near full employment.

The second scenario is interesting because it doesn't require artificial intelligence to fail. It requires it to work.

"An SI-driven expansion could bring substantial labor market disruption as some jobs are displaced, rather than complemented, by technology," Warsh said. "GDP growth would still rise above its long-term average. But widespread automation, combined with labor market frictions, could push the jobless rate above levels consistent with full employment."

The third is a letdown, where "SI could disappoint." After heavy investment and the strong market rally we've seen, returns on capital are lower than expected, a concern some investors already share. This leads to a decline in valuations, and the economy slows before eventually recovering without the productivity gains.

Again, these aren't predictions from Warsh, who has tried to avoid any kind of forward guidance since becoming chair. Warsh also noted, "many other outcomes -- probably including one that will prove most accurate -- could be drawn."

Still, if the Fed is beginning to model these three scenarios, it's likely at least somewhat on the minds of Warsh and the rest of the Fed's board. Interestingly, the first and third scenarios are easier to understand because they are more black-and-white. Either AI works, benefiting the economy, or it doesn't, and the economy and likely the stock market struggle.

But it's the second that I find most interesting, and likely the one most investors are not factoring in.

A successful AI boom that leaves workers behind is harder to see coming and likely harder to model. It's also one that could make it difficult for the Fed to manage its two goals of stable prices and maximum employment, because this scenario could push them in different directions.

If AI delivers on its promise, companies will use it to do more with fewer people. This would lift margins and productivity in the near term, which is exactly what bulls are counting on, and may already be happening this year.

But the people who lose their jobs or are forced to switch to lower-paying jobs are also consumers. Consumer spending accounts for more than two-thirds of the U.S. economy.

Fewer or lower paychecks, or less confidence in keeping them, means less spending power. Over time, that could hit the revenue of the same companies that saved money by cutting costs.

AI investment is already a major source of growth. Business equipment spending jumped nearly 21% in the third quarter, helping lift GDP growth to 3.4%, according to Reuters. But this doesn't replace the spending of millions of households.

Nobody knows what will happen. Everything may work out, but investors should remember that forecasts rarely pan out as expected. The best case for AI isn't necessarily the best case for the consumers who power the economy.

Long-term investors should remain invested, but it's important to consider these scenarios and make sure your portfolio is prepared.

#2917. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2916. I Rate Buy Now, Pay Later Apps; Here’s My Holiday Advice

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

“Buy now, pay later” can help stretch your holiday budget further, but pick your BNPL app carefully and avoid loan stacking.

Head of Content, Personal & Student Loans

As Americans compile their shopping lists ahead of the holiday season, I’ve been compiling my own kind of list — a list of 25 data points, gathered across six “buy now, pay later” (BNPL) apps, fed into a rubric of five categories and 22 subcategories, to produce a star rating for each app.

It’s a big list. I check it twice (or 10 times).

By the end, I know more about these apps than anyone should, and I’m eager to bestow my knowledge on the next unsuspecting person who utters “Klarna” in my vicinity.

I should have plenty of opportunities this year. Nearly 2 in 5 holiday spenders (38%) plan to use BNPL, according to NerdWallet’s holiday spending report. Concerningly, the same report found that a higher share of BNPL users expect they won’t be able to make their payments in full and on time compared to credit card users.

Using BNPL for holiday shopping isn’t inherently bad, but there are risks, including sneaky fees and the ease of loan stacking across many providers.

Here’s how to minimize those risks and set yourself up for success this holiday season.

Brenton Harrison, a certified financial planner in Nashville, says many of his clients are turning to buy now, pay later in the “messy middle of life,” when everyday expenses are high and budgets are tight. This is especially true during the holidays.

“They have kids they're providing for, or they’re trying to go travel and see family,” Harrison says. “When those expenses pile up, it can be very attractive to be able to spread out payments.”

Since BNPL divides your total purchase into smaller installments, typically due every two weeks, it feels easier on your cash flow than having to pay all at once.

While Harrison is open to his clients using BNPL, he emphasizes how important it is to understand the terms before opting in. BNPL apps only require a soft credit pull and a short application. When you can apply for a loan in just a few clicks, it’s easy to miss when payments are due or whether they’ll include interest.

But if clients understand the terms and are certain they can make the payments, Harrison says BNPL's set repayment schedule can be a better option than more open-ended financing.

“With a credit card, [a holiday purchase] might be something that they buy over and over and over again, because that interest just continues to accrue over time, and they never pay it off,” he says.

Not all BNPL apps are created equal. I measure the top apps across multiple categories, but I weigh affordability the highest. My thinking is if you’re already strapped for cash, it’s a bad idea to pay extra just to break up the payments.

There are providers, like Affirm and PayPal, that don’t charge interest with a common pay-in-four term (your total purchase, divided into four installments). They also charge no fees, including no late fees for missed payments.

Compare that to apps, like Sezzle and Zip, that may charge an automatic service or origination fee with your first installment, as well as late fees.

Long-term BNPL financing, which can stretch months or even years, usually includes interest, expressed as an annual percentage rate or APR. This is a more expensive payment option than a zero-interest pay-in-four.

Pay close attention to your checkout screen to see what you’re offered. Are the installment amounts equal or is the first installment higher than the others? That may mean there’s a fee. Do you see an APR listed? That means you’re paying interest.

Harrison points out that just because a store partners with a BNPL company doesn’t mean that’s your only option. Download a different BNPL app, search for the store and see if you get access to better terms, like a 0% offer.

Taking out multiple loans with different BNPL providers is known as “loan stacking,” and it’s risky.

Because you’re juggling different payment amounts due on different days, it’s hard to keep track of what’s coming out of your bank account. This is especially true if you set up automatic payments, which most BNPL apps require. If you overdraw your checking account, your bank will likely charge a fee.

According to research published in August by the Federal Reserve, 11% percent of BNPL users had a BNPL payment trigger an overdraft or nonsufficient funds fee from their bank in the prior year. People with less savings were even more likely to pay fees.

Before applying for multiple BNPL loans, look at a typical month of expenses and ask if there’s extra room in your budget for those payments, Harrison says.

“If you’re just assuming that because you’re spreading it out that you’ll be able to pay it, that's where I think people can get in trouble,” he says. “They set it and forget it, and they don't realize, ‘Oh, I am in no better position to pay that next month than I am this month, or I have three of these going at the same time, and collectively, it's more than I can manage.’”

Most apps send payment reminders, so you can potentially reschedule the payment or change your payment method.

Still, it’s best to avoid the headache to begin with. If you’re feeling pressured to spend more this holiday season, communicate your limits clearly with loved ones, Harrison says, and avoid any loans you can’t comfortably repay.

“Think about the purchase itself, especially around the holidays,” he says. “There's a big difference between something that I can pay off over three months and something that I should have never been buying in the first place.”

#2906. Colgate-Palmolive vs. McDonald's: Which Consumer Goods Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Two consumer goods company giants, one leaning on Walmart and the other on thousands of franchisees, offer very different routes to steady returns.

By Lawrence Rothman, CFA – Updated Nov 23, 2026 at 5:11PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Colgate-Palmolive ›

When market volatility rises, many investors turn toward large and established companies for stability. Choosing between Colgate-Palmolive (CL -0.79%) and McDonald's (MCD +1.15%) requires weighing a household goods leader against a global fast-food powerhouse.

Colgate-Palmolive focuses on essential household items like toothpaste and soap, offering stability through products people buy regardless of the economy. McDonald's dominates the quick-service restaurant industry by leveraging a massive franchise network that generates recurring rent and royalty income. While one provides daily necessities and the other offers affordable convenience, both represent mature businesses that investors often compare for long-term consistency.

Colgate-Palmolive operates as a global leader in segments like oral care, home care, and pet nutrition through brands such as Colgate and Hill's. These essential products are sold in over 200 countries, making the company a prominent name among consumer staples stocks. Investors should note that nearly 11% of its 2025 sales came from Walmart (WMT +1.27%), creating a customer concentration that gives the retailer significant bargaining power over trade discounts.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, revenue reached $21.3 billion, which represents a growth rate of approximately 5.9% compared to the prior 12-month period. The company reported net income of $2.2 billion during this same period as it navigated a complex global environment. This performance reflects top-line expansion across its various geographic regions even as it faces competition from smaller, digital-first brands.

McDonald's utilizes a franchise-heavy business model where roughly 95% of its 45,000 restaurants are managed by independent owners. These franchisees provide the company with steady streams of rent and royalties, which support a high net margin.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, revenue reached $27.8 billion, representing a growth rate of roughly 5.8% over the previous 12-month period. The company recorded net income of $9.0 billion. McDonald's reported a net margin of 32.4% during this time frame, illustrating the profitability inherent in its franchise-led structure.

Colgate-Palmolive faces significant risks from its global footprint, as geopolitical instability in regions like the Middle East can disrupt operations. The company also deals with intense competition from private labels. Additionally, evolving global regulations concerning environmental impact and product safety require ongoing investment to ensure compliance.

McDonald's depends on the financial health of its franchisees to execute its expansion plans, and any broad economic downturn could impact its royalty streams. The business is also susceptible to reputational damage from food safety incidents and recent class action lawsuits regarding product labeling and marketing claims.

McDonald's carries a lower Forward P/E, while Colgate-Palmolive appears more attractive based on its P/S ratio, which compares market value to sales over the past twelve months.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

While neither company is a fast grower, they have generated positive sales and earnings gains. That's positive for mature companies.

Additionally, both have made strong commitments to paying dividends to shareholders. That's an important component of their total returns. In fact, Colgate and McDonald's have increased payments annually for 63 and 50 straight years, respectively. That makes them Dividend Kings, or companies that have raised dividends for at least 50 consecutive years.

Both have sufficient earnings to support dividends, too. Colgate-Palmolive has a payout ratio, or dividends divided by earnings, of 82%. Meanwhile, McDonald's has a 59% payout ratio.

Turning to the dividend yields. McDonald's 2.8% yield surpasses Colgate's 2.2% yield.

The decision comes down to the underlying businesses and dividends. With McDonald's higher dividend yield and asset-light business conducive to free cash flow generation, that stock is my choice over Colgate-Palmolive.

#2905. Etihad’s New Credit Cards Are Primarily for Status Chasers

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site or when you click to or take an action on their website. However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

The new cards offer perks like faster paths to status and elevated earning rates when purchasing tickets with partners.

Etihad Guest, the loyalty program of Abu Dhabi-based Etihad Airways, is expanding its U.S. presence by introducing two new credit cards. Most people won’t get much value from these cards, but they can make sense for Etihad frequent flyers who want elite status.

The two cards are the Etihad Guest Visa Signature® Card ($99 annual fee) and the Etihad Guest Premier Visa Signature® Card ($499 annual fee). Issued by the First National Bank of Omaha, both offer faster pathways to earning and maintaining elite status and the ability to earn extra miles on Etihad purchases.

They also offer extra miles on international transactions (neither has foreign transaction fees) and purchases with Etihad’s partner airlines, which include U.S. carriers like American Airlines and JetBlue Airways.

Here’s what you need to know about the cards.

The Etihad Guest Visa Signature® Card comes with a $99 annual fee, and it earns Etihad Guest miles at the following rates:

3 miles per $1 spent on Etihad purchases.

2 miles per $1 spent on Etihad partner airline purchases.

2 miles per $1 spent on non-U.S. purchases.

1 mile per $1 spent on all other U.S. purchases.

Welcome offer: Through Feb. 3, 2027, the card is offering the following welcome offer for new applicants: Earn up to 40,000 welcome Etihad Guest Miles, including 10,000 miles after the first purchase and an additional 30,000 miles after spending $3,000 within the first three billing cycles after account opening.

Elite status benefits: You’ll also get Etihad Silver status after your first Etihad purchase within 90 days of opening your account. Unlike most airlines, that entry-level status is enough to get you into the business class lounge when traveling through Etihad’s hub at Abu Dhabi’s Zayed International Airport.

You can retain that status by spending $20,000 a year on the card, or $3,000 a year on Etihad purchases.

The Etihad Guest Premier Visa Signature® Card comes with a $499 annual fee, and it earns Etihad Guest miles at the following rates:

5 miles per $1 spent on Etihad purchases.

3 miles per $1 spent on Etihad partner airline purchases.

2 miles per $1 spent on non-U.S. purchases.

1 mile per $1 spent on all other U.S. purchases.

The only differences in rewards between the premium and standard cards are increased earning rates for eligible airline purchases.

Welcome offer: Through Feb. 3, 2027, the premium card offers a higher welcome offer compared to the standard card: Earn up to 60,000 welcome Etihad Guest Miles, including 20,000 miles after the first purchase and 40,000 miles after spending $7,000 within the first three billing cycles after account opening.

Elite status benefits: The premium card offers a faster track to elite status: automatic Silver status upon card approval and Gold status after your first Etihad purchase within 90 days of opening your account.

To maintain Gold status, you’ll need to spend $40,000 a year on the card, or $8,000 on Etihad purchases. Otherwise, you’ll have Silver status.

Wi-Fi benefit: After your first Etihad purchase, you’ll get 3,000 Etihad Guest miles, enough to cover Wi-Fi costs on a long-haul Etihad flight. You’ll then get those miles again after each cardholder anniversary.

Travel benefits: The card offers a statement credit for Global Entry or TSA PreCheck and select travel protections. If you already have a premium travel card, you might already have access to these perks.

The card also includes a Priority Pass lounge access membership. However, an Etihad spokesperson told NerdWallet that it’s the entry-level membership tier, so you’ll need to pay for yourself and guests each time you visit a Priority Pass lounge. Most premium credit cards in the U.S. that offer Priority Pass allow complimentary visits for at least the cardholder.

The Etihad cards make the most sense for Etihad’s frequent flyers who are flying to or connecting through Abu Dhabi several times a year.

That’s because the standard card’s accelerated pathway to Etihad Guest’s Silver status tier can unlock the ability to choose business class lounge access in Abu Dhabi as a status perk.

With the premium card, you get a fast track to Gold. If you select lounge access as a Gold status perk, you can not only bring a guest on the same flight into the lounge, but also access Etihad’s worldwide lounge network. That includes Etihad’s own lounges in New York and Washington, D.C., as well as most partner lounges at other Etihad destinations.

Bear in mind that while Etihad has many partnerships with other airlines, it’s not part of an alliance, so you don’t get lounge benefits when traveling on partners. You’ll need to be flying on an Etihad-operated flight to get into the lounge.

If you primarily fly with other carriers, then Etihad status will have limited upside for you.

However, the Priority Pass lounge membership that comes with the premium card does not have that restriction. You can access Priority Pass lounges while flying on any airline, although you’ll need to pay an additional per-person $35 fee each visit.

The earning rates on the Etihad cards are lackluster for everyday spenders. The bonus categories are for airline purchases with Etihad and its partners, as well as international spending.

That means your day-to-day purchases at home, like groceries, dining out and gas, earn only 1 mile per $1 spent.

Plus, there are more ways to earn Etihad Guest miles than spending on an Etihad credit card. For example, you can transfer points to Etihad Guest from Bilt Rewards, Capital One and Citi ThankYou.

Those programs offer cards that typically have better benefits and bonus categories, and they offer flexibility on how you can use your points and miles. With an Etihad card, you can only earn Etihad Guest miles, which expire after 18 months without qualifying flight activity. (If you’re not traveling on Etihad or its partners at least once every 18 months, then you definitely should not get the cards.)

One of the most interesting perks of the new cards is the bonus category for spending with Etihad partner airlines — 2 miles per $1 for the standard card and 3 miles per $1 for the premium version. Typically, airline credit cards reward customers only for spending on the airline, not partners.

There are better cards available, though. Some offer as many or even more points per $1 on flights purchased directly from airlines.

If you want the card solely to earn more Etihad Guest miles, then the answer for most people is no.

While you can earn more miles for purchases with Etihad and partner airlines, the earning rates aren’t attractive enough to justify the annual fees, especially when compared with existing cards with currencies that transfer to Etihad Guest.

Status chasers could benefit from the fast track to elite status — but you would need to be a fairly frequent Etihad flyer to really take advantage of your elite benefits.

#2900. Cybersecurity Practices That Can Create A False Sense Of Security

Organizations invest significant time and resources in cybersecurity measures designed to protect their systems, data and operations. However, even practices that seem responsible can create a false sense of security—especially when they’re treated as substitutes for more comprehensive protection and ongoing risk assessment.

For tech leaders, the challenge is recognizing when established security practices are effectively reducing risk and when they may be simply creating the appearance of preparedness. Here, Forbes Technology Council members highlight common cybersecurity practices and beliefs that can give organizations misplaced confidence and explain what to do instead.

“We have 24/7 monitoring” is not the same as, “We will catch an issue at 2 a.m.” This is truly a false sense of security unless your teams have fine-tuned alerts very well over time for your situations and context. That means TP/FP are identified, and actual signals are not lost in the high-volume alert noise. To test, inject a true alert at an inconvenient hour and see if ops is detecting it and acting upon it. Monitoring is mostly a comfort unless you make it a security control. - Pradeep Sandhu, Cywarden, Inc.

The fundamental assumption behind traditional cybersecurity is that all the technology in your security stack will behave exactly as designed, requiring little oversight. This could not be further from the truth. Constant governance and visibility into your security system is no longer an option. It is a necessity that can’t be overlooked or underestimated, and it’s a critical issue that must be addressed, especially as we navigate the uncharted territory of AI. - Kevin Lynch, Optiv

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

Prioritizing security by a generic risk model describes the average organization, which does not exist. One bank had 362 vulnerabilities under attack in its network that global data never saw. A score trained on everyone is a statement about no one. - Michael Roytman, Empirical Security

As a CISO, I’ve sat through HiTrust, SOC 2 and PCI audits, and I value them. But a clean report is a snapshot of scoped controls and evidence chosen for an auditor to see, not proof we’d stop a real attacker. The danger is when “we passed” becomes the finish line and the board sees a green light. I treat audits as the floor, then measure what matters: real prevention, detection and remediation speed. - Lane Sullivan, Concentric AI

Many organizations mistake end-to-end encryption for security. Encryption answers one question: Can someone read the message in transit? It says nothing about who is on the other end or whether a record survives. Further, the same encryption that keeps attackers out can end up keeping the firm out, too. Security means governing the conversation at the source, with verified identities, managed access and a record the firm owns and can act on. - Dima Gutzeit, LeapXpert

Treating an approved business application as permanently trusted can create unchecked risk. Vendors now add AI agents and automation to software enterprises already use, giving new capabilities access to existing data, permissions and workflows. Security teams need to reassess what those applications can do as features change, not rely on the risk profile established when they were procured. - Ofer Klein, Reco

Software from an approved vendor, known repository, signed package or internal developer can still contain malicious or unintended behavior. Source and provenance are important, but they do not show what the finished code will actually do. Organizations should verify behavior before execution, not inherit trust from where the code came from. - Ken Ammon, CodeHunter

Early in my career, the problem was, “We don’t know what we have.” Today, most teams have dashboards full of alerts and findings, but visibility doesn’t equal security. If the data from your environment is not contextualized into actionable, cross-domain insights and proactive remediation workflows, you’ve just documented your exposure in more detail rather than successfully mitigating it. - Sivan Tehila, Onyxia Cyber

A common misconception is that if an email passes through a security gateway, it must be safe. While traditional gateways catch known threats, they’re less effective against AI-driven attacks that don’t rely on malicious links or attachments. These newer attacks look and sound legitimate, making them harder to spot. That’s why organizations must pay attention to communication patterns. - Eyal Benishti, IRONSCALES

A green dashboard does not truly mean your business is secure. No alerts may signal gaps in visibility, not an absence of threats. Organizations need security that connects signals across their environment, backed by experts who monitor, investigate and respond 24/7. Regularly test your defenses to confirm teams can detect suspicious activity and act quickly to contain threats before they do harm. - Neal Bradbury, Barracuda

One of the biggest misconceptions about open-source security is that finding vulnerabilities means you’re securing your software. Scanners and AI agents can flag thousands of risks, but without a clear remediation path, they create backlogs instead of protection. For end-of-life open-source software, the stakes are even higher. When official patches stop, vulnerabilities don’t. - Aaron Mitchell, HeroDevs

A dangerous belief is that more security tools automatically mean better security. In reality, too many disconnected tools can create blind spots, duplicate alerts and unclear ownership. A mature security posture is not defined by how much technology has been purchased but by how quickly the organization can detect, understand and contain a real threat. - Dr. Chiranjiv Roy, C5i.ai

Vendor security questionnaires feel thorough, but they capture a one-time self-assessment from direct suppliers and say little about the vendors behind them. Risk changes constantly and often sits several tiers deep, so treat these questionnaires as a starting point, then pair them with continuous monitoring of the extended sub-tier supplier network for proactive supplier risk mitigation. - Yardley Pohl, interos.ai

Poorly executed phishing tests can do far more to damage employee trust than they will ever benefit an organization’s cybersecurity capability. Activities that penalize individuals, don’t result in meaningful and engaged education, or become so frequent that they annoy and create resentment do little to strengthen your cyber defenses. - Kim Bozzella, Protiviti

I think relying too heavily on periodic security audits can create a false sense of confidence. They’re valuable, but technology changes quickly, especially as teams introduce new tools and AI into their workflows. I’ve learned that it’s important to keep reviewing how systems are actually being used, who has access and where new risks may be developing between audits. - Deniz Gezgin, Eightpoint

Mandatory password rotation is often mistaken for effective cybersecurity. Requiring employees to change passwords every 60 to 90 days can encourage predictable variations and credential reuse, potentially increasing security risks. Organizations should prioritize phishing-resistant multifactor authentication, compromised-credential detection and risk-based access controls over routine password expiration. - Gouri Sankar Dash, Tata Consultancy Services

Treating successful backups as proof of recovery can create false confidence. A backup job can finish successfully while a full restoration still fails or takes too long. Test recovery of a complete business workflow in an isolated environment, including its dependencies, and measure the time until people can actually use it. - Shahen Minasyan, Twin Peaks Inc. (TPI)

Access and authentication controls are paramount to any internal system, but with AI agents now working alongside humans, impersonation is becoming a growing threat. These tools often rely on being able to access the same files and systems you can access. It may be productive, but left unattended, it’s risky. Building in systemic limitations to what AI agents can do on your behalf is an important consideration. - Matt Wielbut, Openly

#2898. Why ASML Stock Popped Today

The AI sector soared as Jensen Huang returned to Nvidia.

By Jeremy Bowman – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:16PM EST

Shares of ASML (ASML +9.28%) were among the winners today as the semiconductor sector surged on news that Jensen Huang was returning as the CEO of Nvidia.

The announcement reinjected confidence in the AI boom, lifting sector stocks like ASML, which finished the session up 9.3, while the Nasdaq Composite closed up 6.5%.

ASML is the world's only maker of extreme ultraviolet lithography (EUV) machines for making the world's most advanced chips.

These are highly complex, very expensive machines, and the company sells only dozens of them each quarter.

It's a cyclical business downstream of broader chip demand, including more advanced chips used for AI. Therefore, the strengthening of the AI ecosystem is good news for ASML, and investors believe AI demand and its potential growth are stronger with Jensen Huang back running Nvidia.

Because of the nature of its business, ASML's results can swing quarter to quarter, so the long-term outlook is crucial for the stock. It already stands to benefit from the shortage in memory as orders from memory chipmakers look to expand capacity, and it should benefit from the continued growth of AI demand, which is supported by having Huang back as the Nvidia CEO.

ASML is targeting revenue of 44 billion-60 billion euros for 2030. While the company won't benefit directly from the return of Huang, investors should look for that figure to move higher as a sign that ASML is benefiting from tailwinds from AI growth.

#2894. President Trump Says He Convinced Nvidia’s Board to Bring Back Jensen Huang

Nvidia's board brought Jensen Huang back after "input from senior U.S. government officials."

By Johnny Rice – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:20PM EST

President Donald Trump told CNBC on Monday that he personally lobbied Nvidia's (NASDAQ: NVDA) board to bring back Jensen Huang as CEO, telling the network's Eamon Javers this morning that he had spoken with the board "directly and indirectly" in recent days and that he "convinced them" to reinstate Huang, who "did nothing wrong."

The crux of the issue seemed to be the national security concerns surrounding Huang's original departure: "They did ask for assurance on the national security threat," The President said, "And we gave them assurances at least with everything we know, and we know a lot."

The board's official announcement this morning echoed Trump's statements: "In the process of reaching its decision, the Board received input from senior levels of the United States government, and based on that input, the Board is confident that Mr. Huang's reinstatement poses no national security risk."

While the announcement led to a massive rally today -- Nvidia stock finished up 31% -- the President's involvement could have unintended consequences for shareholders.

The national security concern stems from Huang's reported affair with Alice Huang, the former CEO of Megaspeed, a Singapore-based Nvidia customer accused of contravening U.S. export controls. Ms. Huang is herself a suspected Chinese intelligence asset, though Beijing denies the espionage allegation. The Justice Department is investigating Megaspeed, so too is Nvidia, which opened its own investigation on Nov. 8. The board said it has no reason to believe anyone had compromised company secrets or U.S. national security.

According to Trump, Attorney General Todd Blanche told him the government "does not possess evidence that [Huang] has engaged in any unlawful conduct" and that there's no reason to believe Huang poses a threat to national security.

Trump told CNBC that not only is he not a threat, but that through his work, he is an invaluable asset: "People say, is it a threat to have him? No, it's a threat to lose him."

Trump cheered his reinstatement, saying: "I'm very, very happy that the company had the courage and the board had the courage to do the right thing, and they suffered for two weeks, and the company suffered greatly."

Given the sensitive nature of all of this, the dramatic reversal of the board's decision would likely have raised eyebrows in Washington under any circumstance. But now that the President himself has claimed that his involvement was instrumental in changing the board's minds, I would expect serious scrutiny from Congress.

Jensen Huang. Image source: Getty Images.

Legislators on both sides of the aisle expressed their skepticism. Republican Senator John Cornyn said on the social media platform X, "No national security risk? Based on what?" while Democratic Representative Jim Himes said in a statement that "Congress still has questions that require answers."

If any evidence of wrongdoing surfaces and Huang is forced out once more, I think the market's reaction will be even stronger on the way down.

#2886. Anthropic Stock Is Now Public. Here’s What History Says About Buying the Hottest IPOs in Their First Month.

Waiting to buy IPO stocks months later is usually the correct move for individual investors.

By Brett Schafer – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:23PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic ›

Anthropic (ANT -1.43%) is officially a publicly traded company. The company sold shares at $83 this week, which then popped to over $100 on the first day of trading before falling back to earth and closing at roughly $89 on Friday, Nov. 21. It is now the 14th-largest company in the world by market cap, worth $1.16 trillion.

Many investors are interested in Anthropic as an IPO investment given its massive growth potential and the disruptive nature of artificial intelligence (AI). But what does the data say about investing in IPOs near their debut? The answer is clear: Investors should exercise patience with this smoking-hot stock. Here's why.

We have abundant data on IPO stock performance dating back decades, which has allowed researchers to analyze them closely. When looking three years out from the IPO date -- which is what long-term investors should be doing for buy-and-hold portfolios -- 64% of IPO stocks underperformed, while 29% beat the market averages. However, a lot of the time, even stocks with market-beating performance experienced a post-IPO slump. This usually provides investors with potential buying opportunities at lower prices if they are patient enough to wait six months after listing.

There are two main reasons for this, regardless of company quality. First, an IPO is generally considered a time for insiders to sell some or all of their positions, not to buy. A venture capital firm, a vice president, or an entry-level employee may now hold millions in Anthropic stock. It is not shocking, then, to see them sell some of their stakes now that the stock is publicly traded, a practice usually done in increments during the lockup period after the IPO. Second, a company and Wall Street generally want to maximize the IPO price at which they sell shares, which can, on average, lead to elevated valuations for investors buying in the early weeks of trading.

Taking all this into consideration, it is no surprise that the average IPO stock performs poorly over the next three years.

Seeing this data, a smart investor looking to buy shares of Anthropic will wait at least a few months before starting a position, which will (on average) lead to a better entry point.

The next question is whether Anthropic is a high-quality business in the first place. Growth has been on fire leading up to the IPO, with trailing revenue now at $35 billion at the end of the third quarter, up from less than $5 billion in 2025. This is an incredible level of growth, as enterprises and individuals alike spend gobs of money on Claude's coding tools and other automations.

Growth should continue in the years ahead, but don't think the AI race is over. Anthropic has many competitors, both large and small, and has to contend with open-source AI models that enterprises are increasingly adopting to lower costs. The question is whether Anthropic has locked in enterprise customers to its AI tools, creating switching costs, or whether enterprises will eventually move to cheaper solutions.

Profit margins are also a big question. Right now, Anthropic is generating around break-even profitability. However, it is about to embark on a spending spree to build its own AI data centers, amid the general volatility of the AI race. A year ago, many thought Anthropic was well behind OpenAI, its main competitor. Who's to say what its competitive position will be like a year from now?

I wouldn't fault anyone looking to get in on Anthropic. This is one of the fastest-growing companies in history, with a product that is indistinguishable from magic. There are certainly risks to the investment, but Anthropic also may be the largest company in the world 10 years from now.

Anyone looking to buy shares should hold off in the first weeks of trading, if not longer. Let the company get through its insider sales post any lock-up periods, which end in May of next year, before starting to build a position. On average, you will find a better trading price in doing so.

#2880. Duolingo's 2026 Outlook: AI Personalization Sustains Growth for 65 Million Daily Users

This under-the-radar software stock boasts a Superscore of 89 from our Hidden Gems Primary database, part of The Motley Fool's Moneyball Database system. Here's why.

By Anders Bylund – Updated Nov 23, 2026 at 5:33PM EST

You sit down with a mobile app to learn a new language, but you soon realize you are not just learning vocabulary. You are part of an elaborate, data-hungry experiment that spans 65 million daily active users. Duolingo (DUOL +1.43%), a Pittsburgh-based company, has turned language education into a high-engagement, gamified machine. The stock currently trades at $152.31 as of Nov. 19, 2026, though it has experienced a volatile year, with a 15% decline over the last 12 months as of Nov. 23.

Our proprietary Hidden Gems scoring system assigns Duolingo an overall Superscore of 89 out of 100, placing it in the Strong category. This score ranks it in the Top ~1% of all companies we analyze, ahead of roughly 99 out of every 100 stocks in our database. The Superscore serves as one data-driven signal for your research; this article balances the operational strengths that drive that number with the risks that keep it from being higher so you can weigh the thesis for yourself.

Because the business is highly capital-efficient, it earns substantial profit on a relatively small base of tangible assets, which is a rare quality that allows it to turn each percentage point of revenue growth into outsize returns for shareholders. Investors are often willing to pay a higher multiple for this kind of efficiency, though it does not eliminate the valuation risk inherent in the current share price.

You may want to keep researching before buying if...

The Superscore is just one data-driven signal worth investigating, not a stand-alone recommendation, and you should always weigh these findings against your personal risk tolerance and financial goals before deciding to act.

Duolingo has been a difficult stock to love lately. It's down 73% from the all-time peak in May 2025, haunted by slowing user growth and unstable profit margins. Skeptics worry about generative AI stealing Duolingo's lunch money, as chatbots look ready to teach anyone anything, all in a phone app or a browser window.

I think that's a mistake, based on my experience in several language-oriented businesses.

As long as these conditions hold, Duolingo will have a place in the online learning market. The lessons learned from language-learning courses have already yielded a hit in the popular chess curriculum, with 10 million daily active users and a solid setup for future in-app purchases.

And with the help of AI back-end tools, Duolingo plans to launch more non-language courses over time.

"In terms of new courses, we're not announcing new courses at the moment," CEO Luis von Ahn said on the Q3 earnings call. "When we added Chess, already AI helped quite a bit. And we were able to add Chess, idea to being in the app, in about 9 months. We can do that even faster now."

The stock might not follow suit immediately, as Wall Street can be stubborn about both market darlings and underrated winners. But this looks like a great time to start building a Duolingo position for the long haul.

#2878. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#2877. Why SK Hynix Stock Skyrocketed Today

The chipmaker rose after reports of interest from a high-stakes bidder.

By Danny Vena, CPA – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:36PM EST

Shares of SK Hynix (SKHY +20.00%) charged sharply higher on Wednesday, jumping as much as 23.7%. By the time the market closed, the stock was still up 20%.

The catalyst that sent the semiconductor specialist higher was news of a high-profile potential suitor.

SK Hynix was reportedly the subject of takeover interest from Japanese investment holding company and tech conglomerate SoftBank Group (SFTBY +19.27%). Chairman Masayoshi Son approached numerous Wall Street banks to discuss financing options to acquire the chipmaker for $1 trillion, according to a report that first appeared in The Information. The price under consideration would have been roughly 1.7 times SK Hynix's market cap of $576 billion as of Friday's market close.

The sheer magnitude of the offer and the potential national security implications were just a few of the obstacles that reportedly scuttled the deal, though it isn't clear whether a formal offer was ever tendered.

SoftBank has made no secret of its ambition to achieve end-to-end control over all elements of the artificial intelligence (AI) semiconductor supply chain. The company already owns data center chip designer Ampere Computing and power infrastructure specialist SB Energy, as well as an 86% controlling stake in chip designer Arm Holdings. SoftBank also holds a position in AI start-up OpenAI.

Bringing SK Hynix into the fold would have given the company a direct pipeline to DRAM, NAND flash, and high-bandwidth memory (HBM). However, in a statement, it said, "SoftBank explores many opportunities, and this one is no longer under active consideration." The wording suggests the deal was, in fact, explored.

So is SK Hynix worth $1 trillion? It's doubtful. A premium of that magnitude would likely have drawn the ire of smaller SoftBank investors, even as Son holds a dominant 33% stake in the company. Moreover, as Korea's dominant maker of HBM, it's unlikely the deal would have passed regulatory muster.

At less than 7 times earnings, SK Hynix stock appears attractively priced. However, the historical boom-and-bust cycle has some investors concerned, and the jury is still out on whether the company can maintain its juicy profit margins while the future of AI plays out.

#2875. Why CrowdStrike Stock Was Down Today

CrowdStrike beat Wall Street's estimates, but its $43.9 billion deal to buy Zscaler sent the stock lower.

By Johnny Rice – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:40PM EST

10 stocks we like better than CrowdStrike ›

CrowdStrike Holdings (CRWD -8.92%) stock finished down 8.9% on Monday, Nov. 23, after the cybersecurity company agreed to buy Zscaler (ZS +17.67%).

It was one of the few tech stocks to see red today, with the Nasdaq Composite up 6.2%. The S&P 500 jumped 4.7%.

CrowdStrike announced Monday that it would buy Zscaler, a cloud security company, in an all-stock deal valued at $43.9 billion. Zscaler shareholders would own about 16% of the combined company, and CrowdStrike shareholders would own the rest.

The deal still needs approval from both regulators and Zscaler shareholders.

The offer represents a 34% premium to Zscaler's Friday closing price; investors seemed to think that's a lot to pay.

CrowdStrike makes software that protects devices, while Zscaler helps companies control who can access their apps and data. Bringing the two together makes sense from a product standpoint and gives the company more to sell to its own customers as well as a shot at selling its software to roughly 8,000 Zscaler customers who don't already buy from CrowdStrike.

The announcement overshadowed a strong earnings report this morning in which the company beat Wall Street estimates for revenue and adjusted earnings per share. Sales jumped 28% year over year.

#2870. Why Digital Realty Trust Stock Moved Over 3% Higher on Monday

The Nvidia Effect was strong that trading session.

By Eric Volkman – Nov 23, 2026 at 5:46PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Digital Realty Trust ›

Investors were more than willing to put their trust in Digital Realty Trust (DLR +3.05%) stock at the start of the trading week. Shares of the data center real estate investment trust (REIT) ended Monday more than 3% higher, on the back of developments with one of the company's most important collaborators.

The No. 1 news item by far in the tech industry -- and indeed, global business -- was the reinstatement of Nvidia's (NVDA +31.48%) Jensen Huang as CEO. The move by that company's board of directors came mere days after Huang resigned his position following his admissions of extramarital affairs, one of which may have been with a person with ties to Chinese intelligence.

This, of course, doesn't impact Digital Realty directly. However, as a major data center operator, the REIT works closely with the world's leading artificial intelligence (AI) chipmaker in various ways. This includes work on hardware design, implementation, and research.

When an influential company changes its leadership -- as Nvidia did temporarily after Huang's abdication -- it can generate fear among investors that its business strategy will change. They worry that a new chief executive or management change will seek better, cheaper, or otherwise more advantageous ways of working with its key partners. With Huang's reinstatement, those fears have vanished -- at least for now.

Should we assume it'll be smooth sailing with Digital Realty and Nvidia from now on?

I think it would be risky to think so, although it's far more likely at this point that Huang stays than departs again. With many tech stocks seeing a lift on Nvidia's Monday news, it's important to distinguish those with good fundamentals and truly strong, durable ties to the chip giant. Those are the companies with potential for sustained gains. Digital Realty, which has lately posted record bookings, is one of them.

#2867. Retirees: Is Now the Time to Ditch S&P 500 Funds?

The recent Nvidia scandal underscores just how vulnerable the broad index can be to tech stocks.

By David Jagielski, CPA – Nov 23, 2026 at 6:13PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Vanguard S&P 500 ETF ›

The stock market has been volatile in recent weeks, with the scandal involving Nvidia and its CEO, Jensen Huang, resigning and then coming back within a few weeks, highlighting just how erratic tracking the S&P 500 can be. Last week, amid the Nvidia sell-off, the broad index fell to below 7,000 -- its lowest point since April. Now, with Nvidia's stock jumping on the news of Huang being reinstated as CEO, the index added more than 300 points in a single day.

The wild swings due to tech are something investors have to come to terms with if they track the index via funds such as the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO +4.46%). While over the long run it can be a good way to invest in the markets, the exposure the index has to tech these days makes it a riskier-than-normal option.

For risk-averse investors and retirees, it begs the question: is investing in S&P 500 index funds still a good move right now?

Through the S&P 500, investors gain access to the largest, most valuable companies on U.S. markets. But these days, that means a lot of tech exposure, as it's been tech stocks that have been rising feverishly due to artificial intelligence (AI) in recent years.

Nvidia, Apple, Microsoft, Amazon, and Alphabet are the largest holdings in the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF. Just those five holdings alone account for close to 30% of the Vanguard S&P 500's portfolio. And that's just among the largest holdings. There are also many companies that indirectly have significant exposure to AI, including energy companies involved in data center build-outs.

Tracking the S&P 500 makes sense for the long haul. But during a period of five years or less, a lot can happen. If a crash occurs, it can take a long time for the market to recover, time that retirees can't spare.

For most long-term investors, tracking the S&P 500 still makes a lot of sense, even if there might be a potential crash or correction on the horizon.

Retirees, however, may be better off considering dividend-oriented funds to track instead, which don't have nearly as much exposure to tech. Some funds don't have any exposure at all. During shorter periods, those types of investments can offer much less volatility and be safer options to hang on to.

#2865. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

#2861. CAVA's 2027 Outlook: High-Growth Mediterranean Restaurant Chain Plans to Double Its Locations

This under-the-radar consumer discretionary stock boasts a Superscore of 83 from our Hidden Gems Primary database, part of The Motley Fool's Moneyball Database system. Here's why.

By Robert Izquierdo – Updated Nov 23, 2026 at 6:29PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Cava Group ›

A customer walks into a CAVA Group (CAVA +6.74%) location, grabs a salad or bowl, and chooses their toppings as they move down the assembly line. It is a simple, repeatable process, but behind the scenes, it represents a massive logistical operation that has scaled to 500 locations while maintaining consistent food quality. As of November 19, 2026, the stock traded at $51.58, reflecting a 13% gain over the past year despite recent quarterly volatility.

Our proprietary Hidden Gems scoring system assigns CAVA Group an overall Superscore of 83 out of 100, placing it in the Strong category. The Superscore is an AI-powered score that evaluates a company's overall strength by combining financial performance, product market position, technological capabilities, leadership quality, and relative valuation. It represents the unification of all our scores into a single score for public companies, with five rating bands: Exceptional (90-100), Strong (75-89), Above Average (60-74), Average (40-59), and Cautious (0-39).

A 83 places the company in the Top ~6% of every company we score. This article pairs the reasons the score is high with the risks that keep it lower, providing a data-driven starting point for your own due diligence.

The company maintains a high return on net tangible assets, which puts it in the top half of our database for capital efficiency. This means it generates outsized profits relative to the amount of physical property and equipment it requires, allowing management to turn each point of revenue growth into higher long-term returns for the business.

The Hidden Gems Superscore reflects The Motley Fool's proprietary AI-driven evaluation of a company across product, financial, leadership, and valuation pillars as of the article date and may change over time. Performance figures are point-in-time. Past performance does not guarantee future results.

You may want to keep researching before buying if...

The Superscore is one data-driven signal worth investigating, and you should weigh it against your own research, financial goals, and risk tolerance before making any investment decisions.

CAVA presents a classic high-growth, high-valuation trade-off. The company is rapidly scaling a popular Mediterranean fast-casual concept with strong unit economics, though its premium stock price leaves little room for operational missteps or slowing foot traffic.

The latter, combined with the Cyclospora outbreak, contributed to shares plunging from a 52-week high of $98.79 earlier this year, falling nearly 50% to its current level. Same-restaurant sales rose 9% year over year in its fiscal second quarter ended July 12, 2026, but fell sharply to only a 3% increase in Q3, as customers were scared off by the Cyclospora onset. Yet these short-term headwinds provide an opportunity for investors to jump in.

While its valuation remains elevated, CAVA's forward price-to-earnings ratio of 70 is at a low point for the past year. Moreover, the company is at around 500 locations, halfway toward its goal of 1,000 restaurants by 2032. This gives it plenty of room for further growth in the years ahead.

CAVA enjoys a dominant early-mover advantage in the Mediterranean fast-casual niche. It's resonating with younger, health-conscious diners while extending brand reach through its consumer packaged goods (sauces and dips) in grocery stores. These factors point to CAVA's stock rebounding into 2027 and beyond once worries over the Cyclospora scare subside.
